(Department of Revenue) 

New Delhi, the 5th February, 1991 

S.O, HI5.—It is notified lor general information that the 
Institution /Association mentioned below and its programmes 
given hereunder, have been approved by tile Secretary* 
Ministry of Environment and Forests, Government of India, 
New Delhi, being the Prescribed Authority under rule 6-AAC 
of the Income-tax Rules, 1962, for the purposes of Section 
35-CCB of the Income - Tax, Act, 1961. 

Name of the Institution/Association 
M/s. Vanarai, 2064, Vijaynagar, Pune-411030. 

PROGRAMMES 

(i) Establishing Coordination offices nt the District and 
Divisional Level. 


(ii) Environmental Awareness campaigns. 

(iii) Tree Plantation Drive and Nurseries, 

(iv) Propagation of Non-conventional Energy Sources. 


(v) Publication and Publicity Campaigns. 

(vi) Ideal villages. 


Both the approvals accorded by the Prescribed Authority 
namely (i) to the Institution under sub-section (2), of Sec¬ 
tion 35-CCB and (ii) to the programmes under sub-section 
(1) of Section 35-CCB are valid for a period of three years 
with effect from 1-4-1990, subject to the following condi¬ 
tions : 


(i) M/s. Van*.mi. 2064, Vijaynagar, Pune 411030, shall 
maintain a separate account of the donations received 
by it for conservation activities. 


fii) The Society shall furnish process report of the 
conservation programmes to the Prescribed Autho¬ 
rity for every year by the 30ih .Tunc, every year. 


(iii) The Society shall submit to the Prescribed Autlionty 
bv the 30th June annual accounts showing total 
income and liabilities and u ^Py of c;ic !' . oI the ^ 
documents sent to the concerned Commissioner ol 

Income Tax. 


livl The arnroval is subject to the continued satisfaction 
U of thoPrescribed Authority and may be w.thdrawn 
with retrospective effect, ff considered necessary.

S.O, 820.—-In exercise of the powers conferred by item 
(ii) of sub<lause (2) of clause ri) of section 2 of the indus¬ 
trial Reconstruction Bank of India Act, 1984 (62 of 1984), 
the Central Government hereby specifics a concern engaged, 
or to be engaged, In— 

(1) rendering financial assistance by wuy of venture 
capita] risk capital, factoring and discounting; 

(2) development, maintenance and construction of roads; 

(3) Setting upon of or development of an industrial area 

or an indusvtial estate; 

(4) providing engineering, technical, financial manage¬ 
ment, marketing or other services or facilities for 
industry; 

(5) providing medical, health or other allied services; 

(6) providing services relating to information, techno¬ 
logy, telecommunication 01 electionics; 


(7) leasing, sub-leasing, or giving on hire or hire-pur¬ 
chase of industrial plants, equipments, machinery 
or other assets including vehicles, ships and air¬ 
crafts; 

(8) altering, orgnamentmg, polishing, finishing, oiling, 
washing, cleaning or otherwise treating or adapting 
any article or substance with a view to ils use, 
sale, transport, deliveiy or disposal, 

as an v ‘industrial concern” for the purposes of the said 
clause.

MINISTRY OF PETROLEUM AND CHEMICALS 
(Department of Petroleum and Natural Gas) 

New Delhi, the 13th March, 1991 

S.O. 832 :—Whereas it appears to the Central Government that it is necessary in the public 

interest that lor the transport of Petroleum from Kandla in the State of Gujarat to Bhatinda in the State of 
Punjab, via Rajasthan and Haryana, Pipelines should be laid by the Indian Oil Corporation Limited; 

And whereas it appears that for the purpose of laying such pipelines it is necessary to acquire the right 
of user in the land described in the Schedule annexed to this notification; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub-section (1) of section 3 of the Petroleum 
and Minerals Pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in Land) Act, 1962 (50 of 1962), the Central Govern¬ 
ment hereby declares its intention to acquire the right of user therein. 

Any person interested in the land described in the said schedule may within 21 days from the date on 
whkh the copies of this notification, as published in the Gazette of India, are made available to the general 
public, object in writing to the acquisition of the right of user therein or laying of the pipeline under the 
land to Shri Kirpal Singh, Land acquisition Officer and Competent Authority, Indian Oil Corporation Ltd., 
Kandla-Bhatinda Pipeline Project, Sector 37—C, SCO 178 Chandigarh.

S.O. 833.--Whereas it appears to the Central Government that it is necessary in the public interest 
that for the transport of Petroleum from Kamila in the State of Gujarat to Bhatiuda in the State of Punjab, 
via Rajasthan and Haryana, Pipelines should be laid by the Indian Oil Corporation Limited; 

And whereas it appears that for the purpose of laying such pipelines it is necessary to acquire the 
right of user in the land described in the Schedule annexed to thL notification ; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub-section (J) of section 3 of the Petroleum 
and Minerals Pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in Land) Act, 1962(50 of 1962),the Central Government 
hereby declares its intention to acquire the right of user therein. 

Auy person interested in the land described in the said schedule may within 21 days from the date 
on which the copies of this notification, as published in the Gazette of India, are made available to the 
general public, object in writing to the acquisition of th: right of user tlrrein or laying of the pipeline 
under the land to Shri Kirpal Singh, Land Acquisition Officer and Competent Authority, Indian 0 ; 1 
Corporation Ltd., Kandla-Bhatinda Pipeline Project, Sector 37~C, SCO 178 Chandigarh.

S.O. 834.—Whereas it appears to the Central Government that it is neccssaiy in the 

public interest that for the transport of Petroleum from Kandla in the State of Gujarat to Bhatinda in the 
State of Punjab, via Rajasthan and Haryana, Pipelines should be laid by the Indian Oil Corporation 
Limited; 

And whereas it appears that for the purpose of laying such pipelines it is necessary to acquire the 
right of user in the land described in the Schedule annexed to this notification; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub-section (1) of section 3 of the Petroleum and 
Minerals Pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in Land) Act, 1962 (50 of 1962),the Central Government 
hereby declares its intention to acquire the right of user therein. 

Any person interested in the land described in the said schedule may within 21 days from the date on 
which the copies of this notification, as published in the Gazette of India,are made available to the genera) 
public, object in writing to the acquisition of the right of user therin or laying of the pipeline under the 
land to Shri Kirpal Singh, Land Acquisition Offioer and Competent Authority, Indian Oil Corporation 
Ltd., Kandla-Bhatinda Pipeline Project, Sectoi 37-C, SCO 178 Chandigarh.

[fio 3101 5 / 2 / 90 -lft WTT 1] / 

S.O. 835 :—Whereas it appears to the Central Government that it is necessary in the 
public interest that for the transport of Petroleum from Kandla in the State of Gujarat to Bhatinda in the 
State of Punjab, via Rajasthan and Haryana, Pipelines should be laid by the Indian Oil Corporation Limited; 

And whereas it appears that for the purpose of laying such pipelines it is necessary to acquire the right 
of user in the land described in the Schedule annexed to this notification; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub-section (1) of section 3 of the Petroleum and 
Minerals Pipelines (Acquisition of right of User in Land) Act, 1962 (bO of 1962) the Central Government 
hereby declares its intention to acquire the right of user therein. 

Any person interested in the land described in the said schedule may within 21 days from the date on 
which the copies of this notification as published in the Gazette of India, are made available to the general 
public, object in writing to the acquisition of the right of user therein or laying of the pipeline under the land 
to Shri Kirpal Singh Land Acquisition Officer and Competent Authority, Indian Oil Corporation Ltd., 
Kandla-Bhatinda Pipeline Project, Section 37-C SCO 178 Chandigarh.

Tehsil : Phal

S.O. 836,—Whereas it appears to the Central Government that it is necessary in the public 
interest that for the transport of Petroleum from Kandla in the State of Gujarat to Bhatinda in the State 
of Punjab,via Rajasthan and Haryana, Pipelines should be laid by the Indian Oil Corporation Limited; 

And whereas it appears that fqr the purpose of laying such pipelines it is necessary to acquire the 
right of user in the land described in the Schedule annexed to this notification; 

Now therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub-section (1) of section 3 of the Petroleum 
and Minerals pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in Land) Act, 1962 (50 of 1962), the Central Govern¬ 
ment hereby declares its intention to acquire the right of user therein; 

Any person interested in the land described in the said schedule may within 21 days from the date on 
which the copies of this notification, as published in the Gazette of Tndia, are made available to the general 
public object in writing to the acquisition of the right of user therein or laying of the pipeline under the land 
to Shri Kripal Singh Land Acquisition Officer and Competent Authority Indian Oil Corporation Ltd., 
Kandla-Bhatinda Pipeline Project Sector 37-C SCO 178 Chandigarh.

Act, 1958 (44 of 1958) anti in supersession of the Goverhraent notification S.O. No. 2473 dated 15th July, 
1975, the Central Government hereby fixes in the Schedule below the scale of the provisions which are 
to be furnished to each seaman as the minimum provisions :—

flour or atta.

where available (c)

Explanatory Note :— 

When a seaman is sick and off duty, biscuits, lea or 
Coffee and sugar shall be given to him with arro-root or 
sago as needed. 

(a) In bad weather, when unable t 0 cook rice and Dal* 

185 Gram3 of biscuits and additional 60 Grams of 
sugar shall be substituted. 

(b) When Bread is not supplied 160 Grams, of flour or 

attn shall be supplied. 

(c) When fresh fish is not available, tinned fish or 
pickled herrings shall be UJpplicd in lieu of fresh 
lish, in the proportion 1:2. 

(d) Muttofn shall be supplied to seamen who decline to 
take beef or pork. 

(e) Out of 395 Grams, of vegetables* 100 Gramt>, shall 

be Potatoes, 100 Grms, shall be Onions, 50 Grins, 
shall bo salad group including Tomatoes and 145 
Grms. Fre*ih Vegetables like Beans, Briujals, Bins- 
sels Sprouts Cabbage, Cauliflower, Ladies Fingers* 
Capsicum or peas or suitable available alternatives 
shall be supplied in order to provide variety. 

As far as possible, Tomatoes should he supplied at leafet 
once a week. The same vegetable.? should not be 
repeated on more than tw 0 consercutive days. When 
fresh vegetables are not aavilable, dehydrated vege¬ 
tables can be supplied in the proportion 1:4, 

(f) Til or Kardai oil for Bombay seamen; Mustard oil 

for Calcutta seamen whenever available; coconut 
or Groundnut oil othertvine. 

(g) Out of 25 Grms. of curry stuff, 19 Grms, should 
consist of red chillies, corriander seeds, turmeric, 
mustard, dry or green garlic, desiccated or dry co¬ 
conut, and 7 grm* ‘Gainm Masala* i e. cinnamon, 
cloves, cardamom, cum nun seeds, black jfapper, 
poppy seeds, mutmes, and mace. When frrtsh 
coconut is available, it shall be supplied in place 
of dry or desicated coconut. 

(h) When instant coffee is supplied instead of powdered 

coffee, it should be in the proportion of 1:4, 

(i) When large fruit such as Papay, Water Melon etc. 

is supplied, it shall be 115 Grams, 

During such periods as may be declared periods of food- 
grains scarcity by the Director General of Shipping, if and 
when rice is not available in the quantities required, the daily 
scale of rice rrmy be reduced in units of 25 grams., and as 
compensation therefore, the scale of other items shall be 
increased per day as indicated below :— 

10 Grams of fresh fish or 
5 Grams of meat, or 
50 Grains of dry vegetables, or 
25 Grams of fresh vegetables. 

Whenever provisions are issued in tinned containers, the 
standards packings nearest to the grams weight as per above 
'-calc will be accepted by the seamen. 

C OLD WEATHER SCALE: 

Tn cold weather, an additional 10 grams, of tea or Coffee, 
10 grams of sugar and 5 grams of condensed milk shall he 
supplied daily. No Lime Inicc will be supplied in cold 
« enther. 

The scale for ‘Cold Weather* shall apply— 

(i) Tn the Northern Hemisphere during the months of 

October t 0 March both inclusive and North of 30° 
N in the Atlantic Ocean and elsewhere Nojth oT 
24° N. 

(ii) In the Southern Hemisphere during the months of 

May to September both inclusive and South of 
30° S.

11, It has been argued by Shri Shukla on behalf of the 
Union that the action of the management in debarring the 
workman for a period of 3 years cannot be held as justified 
as the said letter was issued to the woikman before expiry 
of three days from the date of receipt of the letter of offer, 
According to him there is no provisions in the PPA about 
debarment. In support of his argument he has referred to 
para 1,4.1 of the PPA, According to him the workman could 
1 1 \c been debarred for posting as HCCC for a period of 3 
vcai'i only if he had been issued a letter of posting Hnd 
he had failed to join at the place of his posting within a period 
of 21 days from the date of receipt of the order of posting. 
On the other hand it has been contended by Shri Rakcsh 
Tondon, the authorised representative for the management, 
that theie is no force substance in the submissions made by 
Shri Shukla, Shri Tondon has submitted that the relevant part 
of PPA is para 10,3 and not para 1.4.1. Para 1.4,1 applies to 
a case of promotion. The posting of a cleik as HCCC is not 
promotion within the provisions of bipartite settlement. Post¬ 
in'!' as HCCC simply entitles the clerk to a special functional 
allowance only. Para 10.3 is found in Chapter 10 relating to 
posting of Head Cashiers in Clerical Cadre. According to it 
if a clerk working refuses to accept the post of Asslt. Head 
Cashier/HCCC/HCCE he shall be debarred for 3 years from 
the date of such icfusal from tbc said posting. 

12. After considering the relevant provisions of PPA J find 
myself in complete agreement with the submissions made by 
Shri Rakesh Tondon. The relevant para of PPA applicable 
to the case of workman is para 10,3 and not para 1,4.1. It 
has been rightly contended by Shri Tondon that para L4,l 
applies to a case of promotion and that the posting of a clerk 
as HCCC is not a promotion in terms of the provisions pf 
any bipaitite settlement. Such a posting simply attracts special 
functional allowance. 

13. Even if it be assumed that para 1.4,1 does apply then 
also the workman has no case at all. Para 10.3 and para 
1 44 will be taken as ieferrinc to the two different stages at 
wfficb refusal on the parts of the Clerk to whom an offer of 
posting as HCCC has been made will be presumed. Para 
10.3 refers to a case when the Clerk refuses to accept the 
offer of ratine as HCCC and para 1 44 refers to a case 
where the Clerk has accepted the offer of posting under 
HCCC but later on when order of his posting of a certain 
place is issued, he fails to join at the said place of posting 
within n period of 21 days from the date of receipt of the 
written order. 

14. There also does not seem to be much force in the sub¬ 
missions made bv Shri Shukla that letter debarring the work¬ 
man from his posting as TIC CC was hailed before the expiry 
of thr^e dav* from the dale of receipt of letter of offer. In 
fact if wc go through the letter of offer dated 25414982 wc 
will find that it is a self contained letter in which it has been 
clearly stated that if the workman fails to give his acceptance 
w'Miin three davs of Its receipt, it would be presumed that 
hr was not mterested in acccptmg the offer and thereafter he 
TVDiild stand debarred permanently from the post of Head 
C;» c h'er TWe is no evidence from t he side of the Union/ 
workman that he had send the acceptance within three days 
of the receipt of letter of offer dated 254M982. 

675 GI/91—17.

L The Central Goverument, Ministry of Labour, vide its 
Notification No. L-12012i586(87 D.IIfA), dt. 20-4-1988, has 
referred the following dispute for adjudication to this Tri¬ 
bunal *. 

Whether the action of the management of Central Bank 
of India in dbmisdng from service Shri Babu Lai 
is justified ? If not, to what relief is the workman 
entitled ? 

2. The admitted facts arc that when Shri Babu Lai was 
working as Daftri in Ranlgatij Bazar Branch Balia of the 
Bank he was served with a memo dt. 19-8-80, in respect 
of some lapses said to have been committed him. The 
memo was replied by the workman on 6-9-80. The expla¬ 
nation given by the workman did not find favour with the 
management, The management, therefore, by means of 
memo dt. 24-11-80, copy ext. W-3, placed him under suspen¬ 
sion with the direction that he would bo paid for his hrst 
three months at l/3rd of the pay and allowances and there¬ 
after at one half of the pay and allowance during the

AWARD 

1. The Central Government, Ministry of Labour, vide 
its notification No. LT2012|894i88‘D2(A) dated 18th 
August, 89 fias referred me following dispute for adjudica¬ 
tion to this Tribunal : 

Whether the action of the management of Central Bank 
of India in not granting special allowance of Rs. 
126/- per monLii to bhri B. P* Singh, for operating 
Automax Machine w.e.f. January' 1987, is justified ? 
If not, to what relief is the workman entitled ? 


2, The industrial dispute on behalf of the workman Shri 
B. P, Singh, has been raised oy the General Secretary Central 
Bank Employees Congress ihereinafter referred to as Union), 
The view of the Union in short is that the workman was ap¬ 
pointed as Telex Opeiator at Zonal Office at Lucknow, 
on 8-4-85, Besides the salary of a clerk the workman is gett¬ 
ing Rs, 145 per month us special allowance for operating Telex 
Machine. The union fuither alleges that for speedier com¬ 
munication, the bank installed another hot line Automax 
System at Bombay with terminal units at dilferent zonal office 
including Lucknow. At Lucknow, the Automax Machine was 
installed in the same cabin where the Telex 
Machine is installed. Accjrdmg to the union, the manage¬ 
ment forced the workman to operato both the Talex Machine 
and Automax System Machine. Fur opqiating the Automax 
Machine, the management given the training to the work¬ 
man at Bombay, ITie operation cf the two machines by one 
and the same person at a lime L not only in human but 
also against the rales of ihe bank. Protest made by the work¬ 
man, to the management for keeping another person for 
operating Automax Machine weic not at all heeded by the 
management, By means of circular No. CO ; PRS : POL : 
8 / : 95 dt, 17-2-87, the bank prescribed special allowi^ic© 
ici operating Automax Machine* The allowance which were 
Rs. 126/- per month war. raised to Rs. 145/- per month 
w.e.f, 1-11-87, The union further alleges that the bank has 
employed seperate clerks for Automax Machines at diffe- 
lent zones* but no seperate cl;"k has been appointed for 
opeialing Automax Machine Zonal Office Lucknow, Since, 
both the Talex Machine ail the Automax Machine are 
being operated by the workman, the union as well as work¬ 
man requested the management for payment of adailipnal 
allowance but to no effect. The union has therefore prayed 
that the management be directed to pay the allowance for 
operating Automax Machine as well to the workman at the 
rate of Rs. 126 per month w.e.f. January, 1987 and at the 
rale of Rs. 145/- per month w.e f. 1-11-87. 

3. The management admit that the workman was appoint¬ 
ed in the service of the bank as Talex Operator-cum-derk at 
Bank's Zonal Office Lucknow on 8-4-85, As Telex Operator 
cum clerk, he was paid in ihe beginning special allowance at 
Iht rate of Rs. 126/- per month which allowance was raised 
to Rs. 145/- per month as per provisions of 5th Bipartite 
settlement. In July, 1985, the bairn installed an Automax 
Machine and with its installation the work of the workman 
got considerably reduced. Further prior to January, 1987, 
Lucknow Zone had 9 Regions but with the creation of-Agra 
z*one, the number of Region in the Lucknow Zone, reduced 
to 5 Regional. Hence, there does not arise the question of 
over burdening of the workman. The question of keeping 
additional hand is not at all required. Even at bank’s other 
centres no separate clci k Is Posted to manage the Automax 
Machine. The claim put up by the workman/union is based 
on misinterpretation of the circular dt. 17-2-87. The Indian 
Banks Association has directed payments of Rs. 126/- per 
month to Automax System Operators Operating Terminal 

The workman was aheady getting a special allowance 
of Rs. 126/- per month, now Rs. 145/- per month as talex

operator. The said circulai prescribed payment of special 
allowance at the late of tL, 21(> - per month Lo those who 
operate the Central Unit ot tne Aulomnx Bysvcm. This cii- 
cular shows that the Central Unit of Automax System is at 
Bombay. Hence, the queslion 01 payment ot special allow¬ 
ance at the rate ot Rs. 21b/- per month to the workman tor 
operating the Terminal Uml ciocs not arise at all. Hie spe¬ 
cial allowance is being paid to ihe workman in accordance 
with the provisions ot para 5,5 oL the first Bipartite Settle¬ 
ment which lays down that in supercession of para 5.2.87 
of the Desai Award where a workman tails wilhm one cate¬ 
gory he shall be entitled to reoenc special allowance at the 
highest rate applicable to him. The very demand of the 
umon/workman to post a scourate clerk to operate Auto- 
max Machine is wholly baseless and unwarranted, The parent 
body ot the union being Jnc ot the signatories of the Bipar¬ 
tite Settlement, is bound by its various terms and conditions 
as such the union is escopped fiom raising the present dis¬ 
pute. 


4. In its rejoinder, the union alleges that the designation 
of the workman is Telex Opcrator-cum-cleik. The bank is 
extracting duties of uutomux operator illegally from the 
workman. The bank is in tact guilty of unfair labour practice. 
No other new fact is alleged by the Union in the lejoinder, 

5. In support of its case, the union lias filed the affidavit 
of the workman and some documents. On the other hand in 
support of their case the management have Hied the affida¬

Machine is the quicker system of sending inussejge. He ope¬

After the creation of Afiia Zone, the number of regions in 
the Lucknow Zone has reduced to 5 regions. There has keen

fact so deposed to by the management witness in para 8 
of his affidavit evidence. There'ore, it has been rightly depos¬ 
ed (pleaded by the management that the work load has 
j educed. 

4, Anncxure I to the claim statement of the union is the 
copy of circular dt. 17-2-b7 on the subject of payments of 
special allowance to clerks operating Automax System. T lie 
bank prescribed, special allowance of Rs. 126/- per month 
to Automax System Operators operating at tcimjnal units 
and an allowance of Rs. 120/- per month to the Automax 
System Operators, Central Unit installed at Bombay. 

5. There is no dispute about the fact that the special 
allowance of a 'Jclex Opel a tor was previously Rs. 126/- per 
month and since 1-11-87, it has been raised to Rs. 145/- per 
month. 

7. Para 5.5 of the First Bipartite Settlement lays down 
that in supersession of para 5.2.87 of the Desai Award, where 
a workman falls within moic than one category, he shall be 
entitled to icceivc the special allowance at the highest rate 
applicable to him. It means that in no case the workman 
can be paid two allowance, one for operating the Telex 
Machine and the other for operating the Automax Machine. 
He could be paid only the higher allowance of the two. But 
as wc have seen above, the two categories carry equal allow¬ 
ance, teh workman is already netting one allowance as such 
the question of awarding the second allowance in addition 
of the one which he is already getting does ‘not ’arise. if the 
union feels that the operation of Automax Machine requires 
higher skill, it should got fixed allowance at higher rate by 
means of another bipartite settlement and in its absence no 
relief can be granted to the workman. 

8 . Hence, the action of th; management of the Central 
Rank of India cannot be held as unjustified. 

9. The reference is answered agunst the Union/workman,

Kuniari Rashmi Khurana, whs appointed as Enquiry Officer,

Whether the departmental inquiry was not held fairly

T may stale here that it was understood by the parties that

8. The first question to be considered is whether the 
chargesheet, copy Ext. M-7, is vague. I have gone through 
it and find that even a lay man could ‘not say that it is 
vague, T therefore find no force in this plea of ihe Union. 

9. The second point that has been raised by the Union 
i\ that prior to the service of chmg^shert the management 
should have held a preliminary enquiry into the complaint 
of Shri N. K. Sharma Inspector Currency Chest. To my 
mind it is not necessary that every case a preliminary in¬

ii the author of the complaint, h Lhe complaint is by an 
officer but unless the circumstances otherwise warrant, there 
need not be any prehminaiy inquiiy. So in the second 
point also I linvl no substance. 

10. Thirdly it has been contended by the Union that it 
was a non official complaint. I l ail to undcrsl ind how a

l orce. 

11. Fourthly, we have to see wliethei the inquiiy was

tives vveie informed about the evidence to be relied upon by

stances, it cannot be held that any piejudice was caused on

documents with the charge sheet. 1 may state here f aJw> 
that in his cross examination, the workman has admitted 
that copies of documents relied upon by the management 
m the inquiry were furnished to him. However, he has stud

Officer. 

12. In his cios’j examination, the workman has admitted

He has also admitted that he was given full opportunity of 
pioducing his defence. Then lie has admitted that against 
the show cause notice he was given personal hearing by the 
disciplinary authority. 

IT Thus from the above evidence I hold that the inquiry 
was conducted lanly anil mnpeily in accordance with the 
principles of Natural Justice by the Enquiry Officer. 

14 Lastly, I come to the question whether the finding 
given by the E.O. ami accepted by the disciplinary autho¬ 
rity is purveise or not. I have gone through the evidence 
led by both aides before the E O. and have also gone 
through the findings given by the E.O. and find that the 
E.O. had taken a very rational and reasonable view of the 
evidence on record, The findings given by the E.O. under 
no circumstances can be held as puiverse. 

15. Ext. M-6 is the copy of replv dt. 10-6-85 of the 
chargesheet given by the w or! man He denied the charge.

16 Ext. W-13 is tV cony of letter dt. 13-5-85 of the

a renuest for furnHiin" the copv of the complaint But at

counter verson in it. However in hT evidence, before the 
E O. he sf , d'” l that Shri N. FJ Sharma Currenev Ches* 
nffierr carried 'nm* maunders Undine simply because of

of his packet which was made good by him insUnteneously. 
He was not allowed any opportunity of verifying all other 
packets, He also stated that when he made a request to 
Shri Sharma for such 'an oppvjrtunity Shri Sharma became 
rash and it seemed that during his otf moodedness he (the 
workman might have scratched hi‘i heel on caching) which

and CSE became furious with Shri Sharma- He even tried

other staff members. Thus the CSE did not behave decently 
with the Inspector. 

17. On the point of quantum of punishment the Tribunal 
cannot interfere. Interference i'n appropriate cases under 
Sec. 11-A 1/D. Act is called for only in cases of discharge 
dismissal and removal from service. 

18. Hence, the action of the management of Punjab 
National Bank in stopping ihe two annual graded increment 
with cumulative effect is held as juvtilcd and the workman 
is held entitled to no relief.

S.O. 846.—In pursuance of Section 17 of the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947) the Central Government 
hereby publishes the award of the Labour Court Kozhikode 
as shown in the Aunt vine, in the industrial dispute between 
the employers in j ok'ion to the managemem of Southern 
Railway Palghat and their workmen, which was received by 
the Central Covenant on 25-2-1991. 

ANNEXURE 

IN THE LABOUR COURT, KOZHIKODE, Kl RALA 
SI ATE 

Dated this the 31st day of fanuaiy, 1991 
L D. (O 4/90 
BETWEEN 

The Divisional Railv ay Manager. Palghat Division, 
Southern Railway, Palghat, Kerala . .Management 

AND 

Shri C. Rajan, 3, o Shinnakuttan, Chakkumukulam 
Parambil, P.O. Kanayam, Via Shornur 
Kerala. .. .Workman 

REPRESENTATIONS : 

Sri K. V. Sachidccuadan, Advocate, Kozhikode—for 
Management. 

Sri P. P. Balan, Advoca f e, Kozhikode—for Workman. 

AWARD 

The dispute between onj ( . Rajan, Casual Piece Rate 
Worker, Shornur and the Divisional Railway Manager, Pal¬ 
ghat in connection with the termination of service of the 
said Rajan with effect from 26-11-1987 was referred to this 
Court for adjudication by the Central Government under 
Section 30(1 )(c) of the Industiial Disputes Act by the Order 
No. L-41032/49/88-D.II {B) cved 12-5-1989. On leceipt 
of the reference in this Court both the workman and the 
management viz., the Railways entered appearance and filed 
statements setting foHh their stand ir» the dispute puisuant 
to the notice issued from this Court. 

2. The brief averments in the statement filed by the 
workman are as follows :— 

This workman was wciking in the Parcel Unit of the 
Shornur Railway Staton from 27-5-1985. He was 
' engaged for loading and unloading the parcels 
arriving at that station. He is paid wages at the 
rate of Rs. 5 per metric ton. His wages are paid 
from the fund* of the Rviwov. He worked under 
the control and supervision of the Railways. Thus 
he is a workman under the Railways as defined in 
the Industrial Disputes Act. But on 26-11-1987 he 
was discharged from sendee without assigning any 
reason whatsoever. Since this workman being a 
permanent employe ui der the Railways, this 
action of the Rail wavs terminating his service is 
illegal and unjust. Even if the Railways wants 
to terminate h‘s service that can be done cnly 
after giving 'his w n*rian notice pay and compen¬ 
sation as provided in the Industrial Disputes Act. 
Hence this action of the Railways is illegal. There¬ 
fore an awaid may be passed setting aside this 
order and directing the Railways to reinstate this 
workman with back wages. 

3. The gist of tlu a^ennenis in the statement filed by 
the Railways are as follows :■« This workman is not an 
employee under the Railways nor as he vvoiked continuously 

for more than 240 days. It is true that this workman has 


worked in the parcel unit attached to teh Shournur junction 
and he was working as a Porter for loading and unloading 
parcels aniving at Shoinur Station. But that employment 
is not by virtue of any appointment made by the Railways 
nor on the strength of any ag<ecment between him and the 
Railway^ since he vvcu>*t as such cnlv as a casual labourer. 
The reason being sometimes Iheie will be a huge increase in 

incoming parcels in the Shornur Station which cannot be 

unloaded by the Lincence Porters appointed by the Railways 
loi this puipc^e aid on si.h occasions the Railways used 10 
avail the service of outoders as ca c ual on the agreement 

that they will be paid wages at ihe uue ct Rs. 5 per ton. 

This workman has worke l in this parcel unit only in that 
way as a casual labourer. Thus there is no employer-em¬ 
ployee relationship between him and the Railways. The 
Rail wav has no conti cl over him nor is he bound to attend 
for work when called for. It is for him to come and handle 
the parcels when cal led by the Railways. This being the 
position the workman is not an employee under the Rail¬ 
ways. Hence the Railway has every light to dispense with 
the service at any time. Hence an award may be passed 
upholding this action of the Railways. 

4. On these pleadings the following points are formulated 
for decision : — 

(1) Whether the workman involve ir this reference is 

an employee under the Railways ? 

(2) Whether the termination of service of the work¬ 
man is valid 111 law ? 

(3) To what relief if any the workman is entitled ? 

(4) Result ? 

5. No evidence either oial or documentary has been 
adduced. 

6. Point No. 1.—The is^ue referred for adjudication is 
the legality or otherwise ot the termination of service of 
a workman by the Railways on 26-11-1987. The case of the 
woikman is that from 27-5-1985 onwaids he was working as 
a Parcel Porter in the parcel unit of the Shornur Railway 
Station and while so on 26-11-1987 ms service was termi¬ 
nated by the Railways without assigning any reason what¬ 
soever. Now it is this action of the Railways that is called 
in question by the workman. According to him he has 
vvoiked continuously for years from 27-5-1985 and he 
was paid wages at the rale of Rs. 5 per metric ton viz., 
on piece rate basis and that this wage is paid from the 
Railway funds and that he worked as a Porter under the 
cortrol and supervision of the Railways. Thus according 
to him he is. a permanent employee under the Railways and 
hcncc the action of the Railways dispensing with his service 
on 26-11-1987 is illegal and unjust. Now the case of the 
Railways is that he is not a permanent employee under the 
Railways t and that he vvoiked as a Porter only as a casual 
labourer. According to the Railways ihe loading and un¬ 
loading of parcels in the parcel unit of the Shornur Junction 
are done by the Railways Licence Potters and on certain 
occasions th£re will be a huge increase in incoming parcels 
which cannot be handled by the Licence Porters and on 
s^ch occasions only the Railway has availed of the service 
of outsiders like the woikman and that the workman woiked 
in that parcel unit only as a casual labourer. 

7. Any way the short question is whether the workman 
is under the employment of the Railways. As stated earlier 
it is the admitted case that the workman has worked in the 
parcel unit for wages at the rale of Rs. 5 per metric ton. 
But simplv because a person has woiked under another that 
will not that bv itself make him an employee and a person 
becomes an employee under another onlv if the employer has 
control and supervision over the employee. Here it is more- 
or le^s the admitted case that the Railways has absolutely 
no control over this workman even when he is working and 
that the workman is not supposed to attend for work every¬ 
day. Or in other words whenever there is increase in in¬ 
coming parceU the Radway will avail the service of outsiders 
and anv outlier can come and load and unload the parcels 
and they will be naid on piece rate basis at the rate of 
P.°. 5 oer metric ton. Or to be more, precise the Railway 
carnot even insist for the presence on every day or anv 
particular day. So also, they are not supposed to work till 
a narticular time and that they can go at any time. The 
cnly condition is that if they handle one ton load of parcel 



[■*mr II—jj? 3 (ii) ] 


w 2 . 1 , 1901 /^ 2 , 191.1 


they will be paid Rs. ,V lliii is in shoit the turns ot the 
mianficments belween the Railway and the so tailed out¬ 
siders who are called lo woik ir the raiccl unit. 1 bus u 
can be seen that even though the workman has worked in 
the parcel unit, the Railway has absolutely no control over 
him while he was thus working. As pointed out eaihci he 
can be treated as an employee under the Railways only if the 
Railway has control and .upeiviMOji over him. As the 
Railway has no control or supervision over him even during 
the course of his employment it cannot be held at all that 
lie is under the employment of the Railways. Jn this con¬ 
nection il is pcihnenl to iilci to a decision of the Central 
Adminisliali\c Tribunal, Madras in I.A. 77/87 and I,A. 
181/87. Tn these two v ascs the Central Administrative 
Tnbunal was also concerned with a similar question viz., 
whether peiNons working on daily wages as parcel porters 
in the paicel unit, Palghat TN., ore employees of the Rail¬ 
ways. Theic it was held by the CAT that they arc not 
workman under the Railways as the Railway has no control 
or supervision ovei them. But however the counsel foi 
the workman referred to a decision of the Kerala High 
Court in O.P, 4300/79 wliciCm the High Court was con¬ 
cerned with a question of teanmation of service of ccitnin 
woihers engaged in coal loading According to the counsel 
foi the workman the dictum laid down in this decision aptly 
applies to the fact of this :aso since in that case even though 
those workers engaged in coal loading were not actually 
appointed by the Railways, the High Court has directed the 
Railways to give these employees permanent employment. 
But on going through this decision I find that the dictum 
laid down in that case has no app 1 tent ion since in that case 
the workeis involved wcic not actually casual labourers 
like the workman here but on the other hand they were 
employed in the coal loading section throughout. Secondly 
m that ca^c the High Court has not diiected the Railways 
to take them back in service as pointed out by the casual for 
the workman and what the High Court has directed is only 
to consider their case for nermanenev rather sympathetically. 
Oi to pul il in other word* in this decision the High Court 
did not i {0 into the question whether they are leallv work¬ 
man under the Railways. Hence in my opinion the dictum 
laid down in this decision has no application to the facts of 
this case. 

8. Thus in short ns the wnikman involved in this reference 
:s found to be only a casual labourer who woiked in the 
parcel unit according to his discretion without any sort of 
control or supervision by the Railways it has to be held that 
he is not an employees under Ihe Railwa\s. This point is 
thus decided against the workman. 

9. Point No. 2 and 3.—Tn view of my finding on Point 
No. 1 that the workman is not an employee under the Rail¬ 
ways the action of the Rdi*v nvs dispensing with his service 
is neither unjust nor imalid and as such the workman is not 
entitled lo any relief. 

Hk Point No. 4.—In the result an award is passed up¬ 
holding the action of the Railways and holding that the work¬ 
man is noi entitled to any relief against the Railways. 

11. This award will come into force 30 days after its 
publication In the Official Gazette.

REASONS FOR FINDINGS 

6. Issues 1 to 3.—Both patlic, have filed documents, 
which have been exhibited by consent. The Workman has 
filed his affidavit in support of his case and the Management 
have not cross-examined him. The latter have not filed 
any affidavit in rebuttal. 

7. The te,\t oF Ihe termination Older is extracted above 
in para 2 supra. Jt patently cuus stigma on the Workman, 
"alleging his involvement in theft and also describing Ins 
integrity as doubtful. I lie older is obviously penal in 
rature and does not amount to ‘retrenchment 1 m defined in 
section 2(oo) of the Act. Sint. Musurkar for the Manage¬ 
ment urges that the Woikman is not cntilled to pit’ss Jor a 
domestic enquiry inasmuch as he is not a permanent cm 
ploycc. The Law is well-settled th it even where a tempo¬ 
rary employee is removed from service bv wav of punish¬ 
ment an inquiry is necessary, in which he is informed 
of the charges against him and given an opportunity to 
defend himself. Ihe in\ % d.n dm 1 sought 10 be retired upon 
by the Management was in the nature of a preliminary 
enquny, admittedly held in absence ol the Workman. Smt. 




1480 THE GAZETTE OF iN&lA : MARCH 2>, 1991/CHAlTRA 2, 1913 ' [Part II—SEc7^(ii)] 


Masurkar urges that the investigation could hot be held 
in presence of the Workman, because the latter had abs¬ 
conded. No such case is made out in the written statement. 
The documents on the record point to the contrary. The 
correspondence shows that immediately on receipt of the 
impugned Order (Ex. W-5) doted 4-3-86, the Workman sub¬ 
mitted 'as many as four representations to the Management 
{Ex. W-6, 7, 9 and 10) on 6-3-86, 29*5*86, 1-7-86 and 

6-8-87 respectively. He further lodged bis grievance with 
the Regional Labour Commissioner in September 1987 (Ex. 
W-ll). There is no substance in the Management’s conten¬ 
tion that the Workman had absconded after 18th January, 
1986. Accordingly I hold that it win imeumbent on the 
Management to hold a domestic enquiry, that such enquiry 
was not held by them and that the so called investigations 
carried out by them were ex^parte and cannot be a substitute 
for the domestic enquiry, in which principles of natural 
justice can be send to have been followed. Issues 1 to 3 
are answered accordingly. Sint, Mnsurkar relics upon a 
decision of the Supreme Court reported in 67 SLR 458 
Slate of UP Vs. Abdul Kbaliq, It is of no avail to the 
Management, as the termination order there did not cast 
any stigma on the employee. 

8. Issue No, 4—The present one is a case of no enquiry. 
The Management have not requested for permission in the 
written statement or any where else to lead evidence on the 
merits of the charges before ihe Tribunal, The termination 
Order dated 4-3-86 must in the circumstances be set aside. 

9. Issue No. 5—■The Workman will be entitled to rein¬ 
statement as well as full back wages, in absence of any 
evidence to show that he was gainfully employed anywhere. 
The reinstatement will of-eourse be with all benefits of con¬ 
tinuity of service, including consideration of the workman 
for rcgularisation and promotion, if he is entitled to the 
same as a result of his interviews given by him in 1^84 or 
otherwise. I make the following Award. 

The termination oi Woikman^ seivices is illegal and 
unjustified and is hereby set aside. The Manage¬ 
ment are directed to reinstate him with all benefits 
of continuity of service and pay full back wages 
to him fiom 23-1-86 til] reinstatement. The 
Management shall nay Rs, 1500 by way of costs 
to him and bear their own,

3. The case of the workman in brief i$ that the workmen were appointed against existing vacant 
posts in subordinate cadre in the category of permanent employee as defined in para 503 of the Sastri Award 
The work performed by the workmen concerned is of permatnent nature and they have been stopped from 
attending to their duties not for the reason of want of work. The management engaged fresh hands in 
place of the workmen for attending the same work after discontinuing the employment of the workmen con¬ 
cerned in this dispute. Management has caused deliberate breaks in the employment of the workmen 
with an ulterior motive to deprive them of the benefits of permanency and protection available under 
the Industrial Disputes Act. 

4. They have been discontinued from the employment without any reason notice, notice pay or 
compensation despite the directives of the said award and contrary to the provisions of the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947. 

5. The Bank management has issued circular, instructions .o its officers regarding engaging person 
in the subordinate cadre for few days and to discontinue their employment on expiry of 75/90/200 days 
and they are not to be given employment there after. Officers have also been asked to engage fresh hands 
after discontinuing such workmen from working. This is unfair labour practice. They are in continuous 
service of the Bank within the meaning of Sec. 25B of the I.D. Act as the cessation of work is not due to 
any fault on the part of the workmen concerned. Despite their representations and conciliation proceeding 
the workmen have not been given redress. The workmen pray that it be held that the action of the manage¬ 
ment in terrainating/discontinuing the services of the workmen is illegal, unjustified and void and they be 
granted relief of benefit of continuity of service, reinstatement in service with full back wages and all other 
benefits to which the workmen would have been entitled to for the wrongful termination of their employment. 

6. According to the management, the workmen are not “workmen” within the meaning of Sec. 2(s> 
of the I.D. Act. They never worked continuously in one year for 240 days excluding Sundays and holiday. 
They were daily rated workmen. Their work commenced with the beginning of the day and ended with 
the close of the working hours. The provisions of Sec. 25B of the l.D. Act are not applicable. Manage¬ 
ment has denied that there were existing posts in the subordinate cadre in the category of permanent 
employee as defined in para 508 oT the Sastri Award. They have denied that deliberate break was caused 
by the management All other adverse allegations were denied by the management. Workmen have 
worked as follows :—

9. Facts of this case arc almost undisputed as detailed 
in paras 2 & 6 of tho award in relation to the date of 
appointment, date of termination and the period during 
which the workmen concerned worked in the capacity given 
therein. 

10 . I have also gono through the documents Ex. M/l 
to Ex, M/89 and the testimony of the witness for the 
management viz. Kaiiash Nath Baijpai (M.W. 1), N. C. 
Sahni (M. W. 2), S. K. Srivastava (M. W. 3), R. K, Nema 
(M. W, 4), D, K. Jain Branch Manager (M. W 4 5), S. P. 
Mishra (M. W. 6), and S. N. Lamba (M. W. 7). 

11. Two substantial points emerge in the mutant case.— 

(1) Whether there was artificial break in service of the 

workmen concerned and as such they had comle- 
ted one year's continuous service as envisaged 
under Section 25-B of the 1. D. Act ; and 

(2) Whether there is a violation of tho provisions of 
Section 25-H of the f. D. Act ? 

12. It is needless to say that these employees Were work¬ 
men well within the meaning of Section 2(s) of the L 
Act and they were retrenched from service as defined in 
Section 2(oo) of the 1. D. Act. Even Tikka Mazdoors 
have been treated an workmen within the definition of I, D. 
Act. (See H. D, Singh Vs. Reserve Bank of India and 
others;—1985 SSC (L&i>) p. 975; Mohan Lai Vu. Manage¬ 
ment of M/s. Bharat Electronic Ltd.—AIR 1981 SC 18 ; 
Management of Karnatak State Roadways Transport Cor¬ 
poration, Bangalore Vs. M. Boraian & another—AIR 1983 
SC j>, 1320. See also Civil Appeal Nos. 3241-48 of 1981 
Punjab Land Development & Reclamation Corporation Ltd. 
Chandigarh Vs, Presiding Officer, Labour Court & others 
decided by Supreme Court vide Judgment dated 4-5-1990). 
Obviously termination of services of these workmen ar* 
not within any exceptions of the pro\isions of law. 

13. I may also point out at this stage that the law bhall 
over ride any instruction's issued by the management from 
time to time and if they ate couumy ic law they cannot 
prevail over the law. 

14. Because both the points involved are intermixed so 
facts are concerned and can be understood and appreciated 
m the totality of the circumstances of this case I advance 
the following facts which are not disputed. 


15. The relevant part of the written arguments of the 
management dated 18-14989 is aa follows :— 

‘Tt is only after due selection by a competitive body 
that the employees could be appointed. The 
management submits thru even though the appli¬ 
cants wero not entitled to uny chance for perma¬ 
nent absorption, an apport unity to face the inter¬ 
view for permanent absorption in the Subordinate 
Cadre was afforded to each one of them. They 
faced the interview before the selection Commit¬ 
tee. However, they were not found suitable for 
permanent absorption. In the circumstances they 
could not have been continued for an indefinite 
period.” 

This itself reveals of the fact that there was permanent 
vacancy through out the period the workmen were in emp¬ 
loyment and they were not absorbed because they could 
not stand interview which follows by itself that other per¬ 
sons must have been employed in place of these workmen. 
In this regard I will also refer to the testimony of M. W, 2, 
N, C. Sahni (cross-examination). M. W. 4, Ramesh Kumar 
Nema (cross-examination last para), statement of M. W. 5, 
D. K. Jain (last sentence of his cross-examination), M. W, 
6, S. P, Mishra (para 2 of the cxaimnation-in-chief and 
cross-examination). 


16. Next question which is material to be decided hi 
this case is whether the service rendered by the workman 
for a period of 240 days was continuous service. The 
definition of the expression "continuous service” is preferred 
wdth the _words h ‘for the purpose of this Chapter” which, 
means that the factor of continuity or interruption of ser¬ 
vice in terms of this definition is to be limited only for the 

purpose of this Chapter viz. for calculating qualifying ser¬ 
vice and payment of com Dcn3di ion under the provisions 
contained in this Chapter. The words ‘'continuous service’* 
occur in Section 25-B, 25-V, 25-F, 25-FF, 25-FFF in Chap¬ 
ter Vi give certain rights and advantages to the workman 
who have been in '‘continuous service” for specified period 
of time. The section further Jays down that the service 

shall be deemed to be continuous inspit of its intrruption 
on account of the reasons added therein and should remain 
uninterrupted. While dealing with Sub-section (1) related 

to continuous service for a period, it consists of two parts 
i.e. (i) uninterrupted service— the use of the words “shall 
be said to be in continuous service” makes the part of the 



U87 


[wl[—V* j(ii)] 23,’l99l/ : «f<2, 1913 


definition exhaustive i.e. all interrupted service means con¬ 
tinuous service, on account of the following reasons viz* 
(a) sickness (b) unauthorised leave (c) accident (d) strike 
which is not illegal (c) Jock out and (f) cessation of work 
he. not due to any fault on the part of the workman shall 
be included in the continuous service 

17. Thus by fiction the legislature has included service 
which may be interrupted on account of any of thp reasons 
in ‘‘continuous service’ for the purpose of this Chapter. 
These interruptions have to be ignored to treat the workman 
in uninterrupted service and such service uninterrupted on 
account of these cases which would be deemed to be un¬ 
interrupted would be ‘‘continuous service” for the period for 
which the workman had been in service (See also Kishore 
B. Dava Vs. The Dean, J. J. Group of Hospitals and others 
(FIR 1986 (52) p. 380) paras 12 6c 13). 

18. In the case of N. Sundcnimoney (AIR 19)6 SC P< 
111), the workman was appointed off and on by the State 
Bank of India between July 31 # 1973 and August 29, 1973, 
it was observed that the intermittent breaks notwithstanding 
his total number of days of employment answered the test 
of ‘‘deemed 1 ’ continuous service within Section 25-B(2) and 
both the sides had accepted thit situation. 

19. While explaining woid^ “actually worked” used in 
Section 25-B(a) I would like to refer the case of Workmen 
of American Express International Banking Corporation Vs. 
Management of American Express International Banking 
Corporation (1985 FIR (51) p. 483). Ihe relevant part of 
the judgment runs as thus :— 

'‘The expiession which wc aie required to construe is 
‘actually worked under the employer’. This ex¬ 
pression, according io us, cannot mean those dayt> 
only when the workman worked with hammer, 
sickle or pen, but must necessarily comprehend all 
those days during which he was in the employ¬ 
ment of the employer and for which he had been 
paid wages either under express or implied con¬ 
tract of service or by compulsion of statute, .stand¬ 
ing orders etc. The learned counsel for the Ma¬ 
nagement would urge that only those days which 
are mentioned in the Explanation to section 25-B 
(2) should be taken into account for the purpose of 
calculating the number of duyu on which the work¬ 
man had actually worked though he had not so 
worked a!nd no other dtys. We do not think that 
wc are entitled to so constrain the construction of 
the expression ‘actually worked under the emplo¬ 
yer 1 is capable of comprehending the days during 
which the workman was in employment a’nd was 
paid wages and we see no impediment to so cons¬ 
true the expression there is no reason why the ex¬ 
pression should be limited by the explanation. To 
give it any other meaning than what wc have done 
would bring the object of Section 25-F very close 
to frustation. It is not necessary to give examples 
of how 25-F may be frustrated as they are too 
obvious to be stated.” 

20. See also O. P. Malhotra ‘‘The Law of Industrial Dis¬ 
putes. Fourth Edition, Vol, 11 at page 1312”, the lelcvant 
part of which runs as under — 

“There couId t therefore, be no scope for argument that 
paid holidays arc to be included in “actually work- 
ed", certain deemed actual working days. Only 
those days which arc provided in the explanation 
could be included in calculating 240 day,s in addi¬ 
tion to the actual working duy*. Tlic Explana¬ 
tion to Section 25-B has included in the expression 
“the actually worked”, ceitnin deemed actual wor¬ 
king days. Only those days which are provided 
in the explanation could be included in calculating 
240 days in addition to the actual working days. 
The meaning of the words ‘‘actually worked” can¬ 
not be enlarged beyond what k contained in the 
Explanation,*’ 


It has further been expressed as follows ;— 

‘‘11 is for the workman to show by adducing lelcvant 
evidence that he has been in continuous service 
for not less than one year under the employer who 
had retrenched him iro.n service and during the 
period of twelve months, he had served for not 
less than 240 days for maintaining his complaint 
that the provisions of S, 25-F were not complied 
with.” 


21. In the instant case, already staled above, the date 
of appointed, date of tci ruination and the period during 
which the workmen concerned worked is almost undisputed. 
Cessation of the work was not due to any luult on the part 
of the workmen as pleaded by them and therefore it shall 
be included in the continuous service. Jhe workmen |h£re- 
lore would be deemed to be in continuous service despite 
the interruption in the semee as pointed out by the manage¬ 
ment. 

22. I will not deal in details with the Other points faked 
by the workmen viz. whether other workmen were given 
job or the workmen had passed test and even then they were 
not employed or various other workmen were employed after 
their termination of service or they were not given chance 
to appear in the examination and interview, but I shall be 
requited to look into them because they are not only rele¬ 
vant for the purpose of this reference but are ancillary to 
the reference. The reference is very clear and within its 
scope as to the validity of termination of these workmen 
from service with effect from tho dates mentioned in the 
reference order and if not what iclief are the workmen con¬ 
cerned entitled to, these points do strike to find out the 
luirness ol the play. In this regard, thero are various judg¬ 
ments and I will cite few. They are Calcutta Electric Sup¬ 
ply Coiporation Ltd. Vs. Calcutta Electric Supply Worker* 
Union and other (AIR 1959 SO 119) ; Firestone Tyre and 
Rubber Co. of India (P) Ltd. V*. Workmen employed re¬ 
presented by the Firestone Tyre Employees Union (AIR 
1981 SC 1626). Thus if certain facts lead to help the 
Tribunal to arrive at a proper conclusion, they shall certain¬ 
ly be consideied as 1 have already pointed out in para 11 of 
the uward. 

23. Before computing work actually done by the workmen 
1 would certainly like to reproduce para 4 of the Judgement 
of Workmen of American Express International Banking Cor- 
poraled (supra) which runs as under :— 

‘The principles of statutory construction aie well aet- 
tled. Words occurring in statutes of liberal import 
such as social welfare legislation and Human Right* 
logisation are not to be put in procrustcan beds or 
shrunk to Lilipution dimensions. In construing 
those legislations the imposture of literal construc¬ 
tion must be avoided and (he prodigality of its mis¬ 
application must be recognised "and reduced. 
Judges ought to be more concerned with the 
‘colour’, the ‘content* and the ‘context’ of such sta¬ 
tutes (we have borrowed the words from Lord Wilber- 
force's opinion in Prenn v, Simmonda, (6) (1971 
(3) A.E.R. 237). In the same opinion Lord WJ1- 
berforce pointed out that law is not to be loft be¬ 
hind in some island of literal interpretation but is 
to enquire beyond the language, unisolated from the 
matrix of facts in which they arc set ; the Jaw is 
not to be interpreted purely on the internal Linguis¬ 
tic considerations. In one of the cases cited bcfoie 
us, that is Surendra Kumar Verma V. Central Go¬ 
vernment Industrial Tribunals um-Labour Court, 

(supra) wc had occasion to say, “Semantic luxuries 
are misplaced in the interpretation of ‘bread and 
butter’ statutes. Welfare statutes must, of neces¬ 
sity, receive a broad interpretation, Where legisla* 
tion is designed to give relief against certain kinds 
of mischief, the Court is not to make inroads by 
making etymological executions.*’ 

24. As 1 have ulieudy pointed out above in para 16 to 
19 of ihe judgment I may clarify 'he position by reproduc¬ 
ing iclc\ ant part of para 14 of the judgment of S. K. Verma 



1488 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : MARCH 


etc. Vs Central Industrial Tribunal-cum-Labotir Court, New 
Delhi (AIR 1981 SC p. 422) which runs as under :— 

1+ 14. On the other question decided by my learned bro- 
thcr I have no hesitation in agreeing that having 
regard to the simultaneous amendments introduced 
in the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 by Act No. 36 
of 1964—the deletion of S. 2 (coe) and the substi¬ 
tution of the present S, 2J-B fur the original sec¬ 
tion—it is no longer necessary for a workman to 
show that he has been in employment during a 
preceding period of twelve calendar months in order 
to qualify within the tcims of S. 25-B. It is suffi¬ 
cient for the purposes of S. 25-fl(2) (ft) (ii) that 
he has actually worked for not. less than 240 days 
during the preceding period of 12 calendar months. 
The law declared by this Court in Sur Enamel and 
Stamping Works (P) Ltd. v. Their Workmen 
(1964) 3 SOR 616 at pp. 622-623 (AIR 1963 SC 
1914) does not apply to situations governed by the 
subsequently substituted Section 25-B of the 
Act.” 

1 may observe here that copy of this case law provided to 
tue on behalf of the workman leaves out the following 
\voids from the judgment :— 

Aluring the preceding period of 12 calendar months." 
Necessary 7 enquiry and action is sought in this regard. 

25. Applying these principles we have only to look into 
the working period of the last preceding year from the date 
of termination of the workmen concerned from service. 

26. Workman M, K. Kathai worked for 211 days from 
2-7-1979 to 17-7-1980 with a gap from 12-10-1979 to 
12-6-1980 and thereafter, after ft gap of more than 5 months 
for 162 days. If gap from 12-10-1979 to 12-6-1980 is ignor¬ 
ed treating it artificial and deliberate than it would be deem¬ 
ed to be a continuous service for more than 240 days pre¬ 
ceding to 17-7-1980. 

27. St) far as the case of H. K. Pande is concerned he 
was employed in the year 1979 from 1-8-1979 to 12-11-1979 
and thereafter from 21-1-1986 to 2-8-1986 with certain gaps. 
Thus even including the non woiking days the continuous 
service would not be 240 days. Thus it cannot be said that 
Shri Pande had worked for continuous period of 240 days 
in last preceding year. 

28. So far as the case of R. K, Dixit is concerned, he had 
worked from 1-6-1984 to 12-9-1984 with certain gaps. 
Thereafter from 5-11-1985 to 31-12-1985 with long gaps and 
thereafter from 6-1-1986 to 6-5-1986 with various gaps given 
therein. He is also said to have worked from 9-4-1984 to 
30-4-1984 and 1-5-1986 to 30-6-1986, for the whole of 
December, 1986 and March to August, 1986. In his eftse 
also the gaps arc so long that they cannot be deemed to be 
continuous one for 240 days in last preceding year. 

29. So arc the cases of ChWmMuij Bisen and Ajay Kumar 
Sahu who did not complete 240 days of continuous service 
preceding one year from any of the last days of break. 

30. But in the Whole spectrum of the case it becomes very 
pertinent that the posts were available for all the time which 
follows that breaks in service were made in such a way as 
to avoid the attraction of the provisions of S, 25-B of the 
I. p. Act. It would certainly come within the meaning of 
unfair lubour practice and this fact is further established 
from the arguments advanced on beh ilf of the management 
6s reproduced by ule in para 15 of my award. 


31. M K. Kathal was a Messenger. H, K, Pande was 
also a Messenger. R. K, Dixit was Water Boy-cum-Cantecn 
Boy-cum-Messenger, Chatiirbhuj Bisen was a Messenger and 
Aiay Kumar Sahu was also a Messenger. What particular 
specialised knowledge these posts require and why they were 
not appointed in the regular cadre despite their pretty long 
services and the plea taken was that they were not found 
fit in the interview and therefor© they weie not appointed 
leaves a question maik to the bonaftdes of the management 
and iegulai enquhy is called for by the superior authority 
in the matter as to under what circumstances they were 
interviewed and they were not selected. 


1991/CHAITRA 2, 1913 [Part IT—Sec. 3(ii)l 


32 I have already pointed out that in the particular cir¬ 
cumstances of this case the violation of the provisions of 
S. 25 H on the flimsy ground that they were not found fit 
in interview cannot be justified. I may also repeat at this 
stage that in para 5 of the statement of the workmen plead¬ 
ing is to the effect that the fresh hunJs were appointed in 
pla^e ci these workmen and employed from time to time. 
Phis pleading has not been challenged by the management 
in their written statement. Thus their retrenchment cannot 
be said to be bonafide because the posts were available all 
the time during which these workmen were employed from 
time to time. It is for this reason that I can say that all 
thcae bicaks were deliberate and artificial keeping in mind 
the observations made hy Lord Wilberforce and not only 
there was violation of Section 25-B of the L D. Act but 
also S. 25-H of the I. D. Act. Every case has to be appre¬ 
ciated in its own circumstance within the framework of law 
and for the reasons given above it cannot be said that the 
termination of services of these workmen was justifiecL Even 
olhenvise also these Tribunal are competent to create con¬ 
tract. The workmen are therefore entitled to reinstatements 
on theii respective posts from their respective dates of ter¬ 
mination. They shall be deemed to be in continuous service 
frdfli that date onwards. But in the particular circumstances 
of this case T will not award back wages but they will be 
entitled to the benefits arising out of the continuous service 
from the date of termination of service and they will be 
placed above those persons who are employed on these posts 
after their termination from service in seniority. The woik- 
men shall also be entitled to compensation amounting to

9. There is no denying the fact that with the chargesheet

There is also no denying the fact that in the chargesheet it

or the defence i epresentative. At page 6 of his order dt. 
1-6-84. the disciplinary aiihority observed that there was no 
provision for disclosing in advance to the charged employee 
the evidence which was to be relied upon by the bank, in 
the departmental proceedings. It seems that it was perhapis 
in the light of the «u'd observation that cross examination 
was conducted by Shri Mahesh Chandra, the auth. represen¬ 
tative for the management of the workman. On a question 
put to him, the workman replied that he did not make «ny 
ar.>pficat ; <in during the inquiring proceeding*? before the 8 0. 
I hat in the conduct of the inquiry, irregularities were being 
committed. 


10 The above approach to my mind, is not supported by 
any Rule of inquiry. It is settled law that before the start 
of the inquiry, the delinquent employee must know about 
the evidence which was going to be relied upon. He is also 
required to inform the employee about the witnesses to be 
examined at tbe inquiry in support of the charges. In fact 
information in this regard should accompany the chargesheet 
and copies of documents furnished to the delinquent employee. 
But as is the practice in the banking industry atleast all these 
things should have been disclosed at the stait of the inquiry 
and copie. of documents should have been furnished to the 
chnrgeshected emplovee. Thereafter, a date should be fixed 
for the production of evidence i.e. examination of witnesses 
hv the management i enresentative. Between the two dates 
sufficient time - should elapsed so that the workman and his 
representative could study the impact of the evidence to be 
lead by the management In support of the charges. It w W 
exci.sc that the workman or his defence representative did 
nut raise any objection It is not necessary that in all cases 
the workman and his defence representative may be conversant 
with the rules of inquiry T really wonder at the observation 
made hy the Disciplinary Authority who happened to be a 
Hwh Ranking Officer of the bank that there was no provision 
for disclosing ‘n advance to the charged employee the evi¬ 
dence which was to be lelicd upon bv the bank m the depart¬ 
mental proceed inns Here the question ia not of existence or 
any provision bill the question is of observance of principles 
of natural urt ; cc Tn Ihe aWnce of disclosure of such evi¬ 
dence : n advance or its disclosure at the eleventh hour the 
principles of natnril iustlre would be iW^ed to have h'en 
violated Accordingly it is held that the momry was not 
conducted fairly and properly by the E O. 

11 Now let us assume that the inquiry was conducted 
fairly and piogcrlv by the management in accordance with 
the principles of natural justice. The question -is whether the

findings given by the E.O und sustained by the Discipl'nary 
Authority and the Appellate Authority can be said as based 
on evidence or not. As already said only charge Nos. 3, 4, 5 
and 7 arc said to Lave been proved against the workman. 
Let us consider the findings in respect of each of these 
charges. 

12. Chaige No. 3 : 

With regard to this charge, there is no dispute about the 
fact that Rs. 350 given by Sun. Maikni Devi for deposit 
in her account were not deposited nv the woikman in her 
account and were pa : d back by him to Smt. Maikni Dcv| 
after 10 or 15 days. There is further no dispute about the 
fact that she had made a complaint in this regard to the 
branch manager. Tn her crost examination she admitted tha 1 
she had tendered monev to the workman when she was in 
a perturbed state of rmnd on account of death of her younger 
daughter aged about 20 >ears. She said that she had put her 
thumb impression on the complaint, when a querry in this 
regard was made by the branch manager. She also said that 
she had no complaint against the workman, 

13. The authorised lcpresentative for the workman has 
submitted that the copy of the Complaint has not been filed 
by management in order to ascertain as to at what place the 
money had been p/ven by her to the workman. It was a 
private affair between the complainant and the workman. 
She would not have given the money to the workman unless

S.O, 857.—Iii pursuance of section 17 of the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), the Central Government 
hereby publishes the award of the Central Government 
Industrial TribunaTCum-Labour Co ait. Ivan pur as shown 
in the Annexure, in the industrial dispute between the em¬ 
ployers in relation to the management of Banarns State 
Bank Ltd. Agra and their workmen, which was received 
by the Central Government on the 28-2-1991. 

ANNEXURE 

Before Shri Arjan Dev Presiding Officer Central Govern¬ 
ment Industrial 'tribunal cum Labour Court, Panda Nagni, 
Deoki Palace Road, Kanpur. 

Industrial Dispute No. 96 of 1989. 

In the matter of dispute between ; 

The Assistant General Secretary, 

U.P. Bank Employees Congress, 

2/363 Namneir Agra (U.P.). 

AND 

The Assistant General Manager, 

Benaras State Bank Limited, 1
